These thoughts just replay over and over in my head, making me feel like I'm going to ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors in an attempt to reduce the anxiety caused by these intrusive thoughts, such as repeatedly checking your work or the medication list?\n\nPatient: Yes, to try and calm the anxiety, I end up rereading emails, medical notes, and server configs three or four times, or silently running through mental checklists. I just feel like I can't move on to the next task until everything feels \"just right,\" even though I know it wastes time and makes me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors, such as rereading or mentally checking, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about trying to get things just right to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both. I feel like I have to do these checks to prevent terrible mistakes, like giving my sister the wrong dosage or pushing bad code to production. But I also get trapped by this intense need to have everything feel \"just right\" to calm my anxiety before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much time you spend on these repetitive behaviors or mental checklists each day, and do you feel like they significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: It easily eats up several hours of my day because I'm constantly stuck rereading everything three or four times and running through mental lists, which always makes me late. It has significantly interfered with my life—I've started missing important details at work because I get so bogged down, and I've had to cut back on my mentoring and community events because I'm just too mentally drained and anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms when these anxious thoughts or urges to repeat behaviors become overwhelming, such as a racing heart, sweating, or trembling?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating when this happens. It's more that my head feels incredibly tense and overwhelmed, leaving me completely mentally exhausted."

It has significantly interfered with my life—I've started missing important details at work because I get so bogged down, and I've had to cut back on my mentoring and community events because I'm just too mentally drained and anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms when these anxious thoughts or urges to repeat behaviors become overwhelming, such as a racing heart, sweating, or trembling?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating when this happens. It has made it impossible to relax or enjoy my hobbies, like hiking or drumming, because my mind is constantly stuck on these worries.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts and the urge to perform repetitive behaviors are ego-dystonic, meaning they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself and are alien to your typical way of thinking, or do they feel more like an exaggerated version of your own worries and concerns?"

Even though they are rooted in my real responsibilities, they feel uncontrollable and irrational because they get so deeply stuck in my head and I just can't dismiss them.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific themes or content to these intrusive thoughts that you find particularly distressing or if they seem to be more generalized worries about making mistakes or not meeting expectations?\n\nPatient: They aren't just general worries, but very specific and distressing \"what-if\" scenarios that I can't shake. I just worry constantly about ruining things for my family and my job through my own negligence."

The main themes are constantly fearing that I've typed the wrong medication dosage on my sister's medical paperwork, or that I've pushed bad code to production at work. These specific thoughts replay endlessly, making me feel like I'm on the verge of ruining everything for my family and my job.\n\nDoctor: Do these specific 'what-if' scenarios, such as fearing you've typed the wrong medication dosage or pushed bad code, ever involve harm to yourself or others in a violent or disturbing manner?\n\nPatient: No, they don't involve any physical violence or self-harm at all. My thoughts are strictly focused on making devastating mistakes and failing in my responsibilities, like messing up my sister's medication list or pushing bad code. I just worry constantly about ruining things for my family and my job through my own negligence.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these specific, distressing 'what-if' scenarios are the primary source of your anxiety, or is it more the general feeling of uncertainty and worry about the future that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the specific, distressing \"what-if\" scenarios that drive my anxiety, rather than a vague or general worry about the future. My brain latches onto exact details, like fearing I typed the wrong dosage on my sister's medication list or pushed bad code to production, and replays them endlessly. It is those concrete, catastrophic thoughts that get completely stuck in my head and cause all the panic."

When his older sister in South Dakota developed serious cardiac and mobility problems, he began traveling frequently to help coordinate her care and manage medical paperwork, all while trying to maintain his leadership role at work. After he missed a critical configuration change at work because he had spent so long mentally going over unrelated worries that he delayed the task until the last minute, and then found himself lying awake for hours caught in the same mental loops, a colleague and close friend from the hikers club urged him to seek psychiatric help, noting that he seemed \"trapped in his head\" even on the trail.\n- Chief Complaint: “My mind keeps getting stuck on the same upsetting thoughts about messing things up for my family and at work, and I can’t let them go.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S065 (Obsessions): It’s like my brain latches onto one horrible ‘what if’—like what if I typed the wrong dosage in my sister’s med list, or pushed bad code to production—and then replays all the ways I could ruin everything, over and over, no matter how many times I tell myself it’s irrational or check that it’s fine.\n- S066 (Compulsions): To calm myself down I end up rereading emails, medical notes, and server configs three or four times, or silently running through these little mental checklists and phrases, and I don’t feel like I can move on to the next thing until it feels ‘just right,’ even when I know I’m wasting time and making myself late.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started missing important details at work because I get so bogged down rechecking things, and I’ve had to cut back on community events and mentoring because I’m too mentally drained and worried I’ll make a mistake.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: My mind keeps getting stuck on the same upsetting thoughts about messing things up for my family and at work, and I can’t let them go. It has become incredibly exhausting, and a close friend suggested I finally come in to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these upsetting thoughts, are they primarily about specific situations or events that have happened, or are they more about fears of things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: They are definitely fears about what might happen in the future, specifically these constant, anxious \"what-if\" scenarios. I get terrified that I've made a catastrophic mistake, like typing the wrong dosage on my sister's medication list or pushing bad code to production. These thoughts just replay over and over in my head, making me feel like I'm going to ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors in an attempt to reduce the anxiety caused by these intrusive thoughts, such as repeatedly checking your work or the medication list?\n\nPatient: Yes, to try and calm the anxiety, I end up rereading emails, medical notes, and server configs three or four times, or silently running through mental checklists. I just feel like I can't move on to the next task until everything feels \"just right,\" even though I know it wastes time and makes me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors, such as rereading or mentally checking, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about trying to get things just right to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both. I feel like I have to do these checks to prevent terrible mistakes, like giving my sister the wrong dosage or pushing bad code to production. But I also get trapped by this intense need to have everything feel \"just right\" to calm my anxiety before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much time you spend on these repetitive behaviors or mental checklists each day, and do you feel like they significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: It easily eats up several hours of my day because I'm constantly stuck rereading everything three or four times and running through mental lists, which always makes me late. It has significantly interfered with my life—I've started missing important details at work because I get so bogged down, and I've had to cut back on my mentoring and community events because I'm just too mentally drained and anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms when these anxious thoughts or urges to repeat behaviors become overwhelming, such as a racing heart, sweating, or trembling?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating when this happens. It's more that my head feels incredibly tense and overwhelmed, leaving me completely mentally exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any periods of persistent sadness, hopelessness, or a lack of interest in activities you normally enjoy, alongside these anxious thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel deeply sad or hopeless, but I am just extremely exhausted and drained all the time. It has made it impossible to relax or enjoy my hobbies, like hiking or drumming, because my mind is constantly stuck on these worries.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts and the urge to perform repetitive behaviors are ego-dystonic, meaning they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself and are alien to your typical way of thinking, or do they feel more like an exaggerated version of your own worries and concerns?\n\nPatient: They definitely feel like an extreme, runaway exaggeration of my actual worries about my sister's health and my job, rather than something completely foreign or alien. Even though they are rooted in my real responsibilities, they feel uncontrollable and irrational because they get so deeply stuck in my head and I just can't dismiss them.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific themes or content to these intrusive thoughts that you find particularly distressing or if they seem to be more generalized worries about making mistakes or not meeting expectations?\n\nPatient: They aren't just general worries, but very specific and distressing \"what-if\" scenarios that I can't shake. The main themes are constantly fearing that I've typed the wrong medication dosage on my sister's medical paperwork, or that I've pushed bad code to production at work. These specific thoughts replay endlessly, making me feel like I'm on the verge of ruining everything for my family and my job.\n\nDoctor: Do these specific 'what-if' scenarios, such as fearing you've typed the wrong medication dosage or pushed bad code, ever involve harm to yourself or others in a violent or disturbing manner?\n\nPatient: No, they don't involve any physical violence or self-harm at all. My thoughts are strictly focused on making devastating mistakes and failing in my responsibilities, like messing up my sister's medication list or pushing bad code. I just worry constantly about ruining things for my family and my job through my own negligence.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these specific, distressing 'what-if' scenarios are the primary source of your anxiety, or is it more the general feeling of uncertainty and worry about the future that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the specific, distressing \"what-if\" scenarios that drive my anxiety, rather than a vague or general worry about the future. My brain latches onto exact details, like fearing I typed the wrong dosage on my sister's medication list or pushed bad code to production, and replays them endlessly. It is those concrete, catastrophic thoughts that get completely stuck in my head and cause all the panic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your fears of making mistakes, such as unwanted aggressive or perverse thoughts, or are they all centered around your responsibilities and potential errors?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any aggressive or perverse thoughts like that at all. My intrusive thoughts are entirely centered on my responsibilities, making mistakes, and the fear of ruining things for my family or my work. My anxiety is strictly tied to those \"what-if\" scenarios about messing up my tasks, my sister's care, or my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these specific 'what-if' scenarios are realistically possible or are they more exaggerated and unlikely to happen?"
  }

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: My mind keeps getting stuck on the same upsetting thoughts about messing things up for my family and at work, and I can’t let them go. It has become incredibly exhausting, and a close friend suggested I finally come in to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these upsetting thoughts, are they primarily about specific situations or events that have happened, or are they more about fears of things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: They are definitely fears about what might happen in the future, specifically these constant, anxious \"what-if\" scenarios. I get terrified that I've made a catastrophic mistake, like typing the wrong dosage on my sister's medication list or pushing bad code to production. These thoughts just replay over and over in my head, making me feel like I'm going to ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors in an attempt to reduce the anxiety caused by these intrusive thoughts, such as repeatedly checking your work or the medication list?\n\nPatient: Yes, to try and calm the anxiety, I end up rereading emails, medical notes, and server configs three or four times, or silently running through mental checklists. I just feel like I can't move on to the next task until everything feels \"just right,\" even though I know it wastes time and makes me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors, such as rereading or mentally checking, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about trying to get things just right to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both. I feel like I have to do these checks to prevent terrible mistakes, like giving my sister the wrong dosage or pushing bad code to production. But I also get trapped by this intense need to have everything feel \"just right\" to calm my anxiety before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much time you spend on these repetitive behaviors or mental checklists each day, and do you feel like they significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: It easily eats up several hours of my day because I'm constantly stuck rereading everything three or four times and running through mental lists, which always makes me late. It has significantly interfered with my life—I've started missing important details at work because I get so bogged down, and I've had to cut back on my mentoring and community events because I'm just too mentally drained and anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms when these anxious thoughts or urges to repeat behaviors become overwhelming, such as a racing heart, sweating, or trembling?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating when this happens. It's more that my head feels incredibly tense and overwhelmed, leaving me completely mentally exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any periods of persistent sadness, hopelessness, or a lack of interest in activities you normally enjoy, alongside these anxious thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel deeply sad or hopeless, but I am just extremely exhausted and drained all the time. It has made it impossible to relax or enjoy my hobbies, like hiking or drumming, because my mind is constantly stuck on these worries.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts and the urge to perform repetitive behaviors are ego-dystonic, meaning they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself and are alien to your typical way of thinking, or do they feel more like an exaggerated version of your own worries and concerns?\n\nPatient: They definitely feel like an extreme, runaway exaggeration of my actual worries about my sister's health and my job, rather than something completely foreign or alien. Even though they are rooted in my real responsibilities, they feel uncontrollable and irrational because they get so deeply stuck in my head and I just can't dismiss them.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific themes or content to these intrusive thoughts that you find particularly distressing or if they seem to be more generalized worries about making mistakes or not meeting expectations?\n\nPatient: They aren't just general worries, but very specific and distressing \"what-if\" scenarios that I can't shake. The main themes are constantly fearing that I've typed the wrong medication dosage on my sister's medical paperwork, or that I've pushed bad code to production at work. These specific thoughts replay endlessly, making me feel like I'm on the verge of ruining everything for my family and my job.\n\nDoctor: Do these specific 'what-if' scenarios, such as fearing you've typed the wrong medication dosage or pushed bad code, ever involve harm to yourself or others in a violent or disturbing manner?\n\nPatient: No, they don't involve any physical violence or self-harm at all. My thoughts are strictly focused on making devastating mistakes and failing in my responsibilities, like messing up my sister's medication list or pushing bad code. I just worry constantly about ruining things for my family and my job through my own negligence.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these specific, distressing 'what-if' scenarios are the primary source of your anxiety, or is it more the general feeling of uncertainty and worry about the future that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the specific, distressing \"what-if\" scenarios that drive my anxiety, rather than a vague or general worry about the future. My brain latches onto exact details, like fearing I typed the wrong dosage on my sister's medication list or pushed bad code to production, and replays them endlessly. It is those concrete, catastrophic thoughts that get completely stuck in my head and cause all the panic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your fears of making mistakes, such as unwanted aggressive or perverse thoughts, or are they all centered around your responsibilities and potential errors?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any aggressive or perverse thoughts like that at all. My intrusive thoughts are entirely centered on my responsibilities, making mistakes, and the fear of ruining things for my family or my work. My anxiety is strictly tied to those \"what-if\" scenarios about messing up my tasks, my sister's care, or my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these specific 'what-if' scenarios are realistically possible or are they more exaggerated and unlikely to happen?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I know these fears are highly exaggerated and very unlikely to actually happen. But in the moment, they feel so incredibly real and terrifying that I just can't let them go. It's like my logical mind gets completely hijacked by the panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any control over these intrusive 'what-if' thoughts when they first start, or do they immediately take over and feel impossible to stop?"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
